AMD taps World Labs' model expertise to shape future chip and systems design.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AMD has agreed to acquire AI model research firm World Labs in an all-stock purchase worth around $8.2 billion. World Labs co-founder Fei-Fei Li will join AMD as executive vice president and chief scientist, reporting to CEO Lisa Su. The deal will be finalized once it passes the usual regulatory hurdles.
Su says building compute platforms for the next generation of AI requires “a deep understanding of how models are evolving,” and Li says advancing the technology “requires close collaboration across model research, systems and compute,” and wrote in a separate post that World Labs needs to get “closer to the hardware,” without which AI is “hobbled in efficiency.”
Before founding World Labs, Li had already established herself as a luminary in AI research and development. She led the vital ImageNet project in the 2010s and has served as the chief scientist of AI and machine learning at Google Cloud and the director of the Stanford Artificial Intelligence Laboratory. Thanks to her many contributions to the field, she has been dubbed the ""Godmother of AI"" by several institutions.
World Labs develops “spatial-intelligence models that generate, reconstruct and simulate interactive 3D environments,” which are useful in robotics and simulation applications. Its world models build or simulate 3D scenes rather than being text-focused like LLMs. The company’s first commercial product was Marble, launched late last year, which creates persistent, downloadable 3D worlds from source material.
AMD and World Labs had already been working together to optimize training and inference for its models, and AMD had invested in World Labs’ $1 billion funding round in February. During Su’s keynote at CES in January, Li said that part of World Labs’ model ran on AMD's Instinct accelerators, and her team used both Instinct hardware and the ROCm software stack to achieve performance improvements for their applications. This tuning experience positioned World Labs as one of AMD’s showcase customers, and AMD says the deal will “help shape its future technology roadmaps.”
World Labs is AMD’s second-largest deal after FPGA designer Xilinx, which came out to about $50 billion at the close in 2022. AMD's other big buys have been smaller: ATI for $5.4 billion in 2006, AI systems designer ZT Systems for $4.9 billion in 2025, and networking solutions developer Pensando for $1.9 billion in 2022.
AI titan Nvidia was also an investor in World Labs in the round earlier this year. However, Nvidia has already been developing Cosmos, its own family of open-source world models. It agreed to buy Hugging Face, a platform for hosting and distributing open models, earlier this month for $12.93 billion.
Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.
World Labs' team will continue to do model research. The companies say that the deal will strengthen an “open AI ecosystem,” per the release, and World Labs said in its own post that it remains committed to “widely accessible open models.”
Atlas, World Labs’ latest world model, will power future versions of its Marble product, the company says. Li’s co-founders Justin Johnson and Ben Mildenhall will continue to work with Li in leading World Labs, including after Li takes on the new role at AMD after the expected close of the transaction by the end of the year.

63,2026-09-30,"In May 2022, I found myself longing for a new life.
I had spent the last 20 years rebuilding my life after my husband's death in the World Trade Center during the 9/11 attacks — raising a 2-year-old and 6-year-old alone, moving across the country to Seattle, writing books about grief, and rediscovering painting.
My kids had both finished college and moved on with their lives, and I was alone and free for the first time since my 20s. I felt adrift.
Then, my daughter showed me a YouTube video about a young couple with two small children renovating a chateau in France.
They reminded me of my husband and me long ago, and how we'd renovated historic houses in cities like London and Boston while raising young children. As newlyweds, we'd dreamed about one day living somewhere in Europe, maybe a small cottage in the French countryside, but it became a faraway fantasy when he was relocated to the US for work.
After watching those videos, that dream came bubbling back — and I realized now might be the perfect time to start chasing it.
I loved the idea of renovating a property in a place I'd dreamed about living while also following my passion for art and connection. I was lonely in Seattle, and I longed to create a space abroad that inspired people to gather, reflect, dream, and start fresh.
My vague idea was to find a place where I could host creative retreats for writers and artists.
So, single and in my late 50s, I planned a trip to France to explore historic buildings for sale. Visiting historic properties while enjoying the French countryside was a perfect form of solo travel for me.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
I decided to look for properties with multiple bedrooms that didn't need overly extensive renovations, weren't far from international transportation, and were located in an area that wouldn't get too cold in the winter. Toulouse seemed a perfect place to start.
For three weeks, I drove over 600 miles looking at chateaux, manor houses, and farmhouses — many dilapidated, or near busy highways or in the middle of nowhere. I spoke with real-estate agents every day and veered off the road several times to peer through the gates of beautiful properties.
I returned to Seattle empty-handed, expecting that the desire to buy a chateau would have fizzled — instead, the flames were fanned. The retreat center idea became more solidly embedded in my mind, and within the year, I was back, visiting more potential properties.
At last, I stumbled upon a dilapidated manor house nestled into limestone cliffs, where the outbuildings were actual caves. After seeing it online, I convinced my real-estate agent to leave his locale and show me the property.
Château de Borie in the Lot and Garonne region in southwestern France, between Toulouse and Bordeaux, had been unoccupied for several years. It had 12 bedrooms and 12 bathrooms, and, more importantly, new septic, electrical, plumbing, and heating systems.
After viewing more than 15 properties over two separate trips, I knew having these updated essentials in place in an older building was a rare gift. I told myself the property was in a state I could manage on my own — it just needed an interior facelift and some furniture.
After a one-hour viewing, I impulsively made a low-ball offer, nearly 70,000 euros below the asking price. Within 30 minutes, we had settled on the price of 610,000 euros.
I was unable to schedule a return visit before my flight the next day, so I relied on the few photos I took to remind me what I was getting myself into.
Back home in Seattle, I questioned my sanity as I arranged to have 50 pages of French legal documents translated.
I took out a home equity loan on my Seattle home, which, with some additional savings, would allow me to pay for the house in cash, since getting a loan in France would be extremely difficult.
To help obtain a visa, I had to create a business plan for my ill-conceived retreat idea in order to get a ""real and serious business"" certificate, a name I found humorous. As silly as this sounded, doing this helped me solidify my vision.
Still, I spent nights awake, now acutely aware that I would be leaving my home, my children, my life back in Seattle. How was I going to manage two houses when I often felt I could barely manage one? Could I run a business on my own? What was I thinking?
Yet, three months later, I was back in France (alongside a friend and translator) for the closing, signing what felt like a thousand pages. Later, with a bottle of Champagne in hand, I found myself — for only the second time — at that giant green front door, turning the key.
The following days and months were filled with cleaning, painting, and battling with heaving shutters during a particularly violent thunderstorm.
At my daughter's urging, I started documenting every step of the journey on YouTube and Instagram as the ""Chateau Chronicles"" — it helped get the word out about my business, and over time, I built up more than 100,000 followers and subscribers across platforms.
I offered my first retreat — a sketchbook-and-yoga week for 14 people — in fall 2023, just one year after purchasing the chateau. Prices ranged from 2,900 to 3,400 euros for the week, depending on the room. It sold out almost immediately.
Attendees flew in from across North America and even Australia to sketch. Some were already artists, others were not, and the week was magical.
We stood around a table full of secondhand store (brocante) knickknacks or in the chateau garden to sketch, we visited other chateaux for cooking lessons and tasting regional wines. Even three years later, the WhatsApp group I created for the attendees is still active.
Since then, I've hosted many things from writing retreats to a supper club with a Michelin-trained chef. Four years later, the retreat business supports itself, earning a gross income of around 80,000 euros a year.
My goal isn't to scale endlessly, it's to deepen. To attract the right people, offer them something authentic, and send them home transformed.
The people who come here are often at a turning point — in their careers, identities, or relationships — and looking for a reset. The chateau has become a sanctuary for people seeking a place to land as they undergo a transformation. They usually come away with wonderful memories and a whole new group of friends.
However, running a retreat space in a historic building means there's always something breaking or needing attention.
I market the business, manage the guest experience, develop new offerings, and oversee ongoing renovations. I've painted most of the interiors myself.
Even a few weeks away comes with consequences — leaking roofs, burst pipes, dead goldfish, and falling trees and rocks. I also struggle with language barriers, finding contractors and artisans, complex French bureaucracy, and geological challenges that I didn't fully anticipate.
However, I've also been lucky enough to find help through volunteers and friends, both new and old. And by now, I know which windows stick, and I have a special broom for clearing away spiderwebs.
What I thought would be a part-time venture has become my full-time life. Although it's hard work, this place continues to inspire me and frustrate me in equal measure.
Things aren't always easy in France, but my life here is rich with beauty, incredible food, and a continuous stream of new friends.
I thought leaving my kids in the US would be difficult — and it can be — but when they visit, I get a week or so of uninterrupted time with them, more than the dinner or two a month if we lived in the same city.
I cherish those times. I even recently hosted my daughter's wedding at the chateau, a truly magical experience.
Reinvention, it turns out, is something I have in common with so many women in their 50s and 60s who are done apologizing for wanting a life filled with joy, depth of meaning, and beauty.
Widowhood forced me to reinvent myself, but also gave me the incredible gift of freedom to make the impulsive decisions necessary to live my best, unexpected life.

""For the benefit of humanity, the markets should tank hard and prevent the OpenAI and Anthropic IPOs,"" the ""Big Short"" investor wrote Tuesday on X.
In follow-up replies, Burry offered more detail on his post. He said the companies would ""suck up"" and then destroy ""TRILLIONS of dollars of capital,"" adding that this would be ""the least of the damage they do.""
Burry also replied ""Along those lines"" to a user who quipped: ""tank the market so Skynet can’t IPO"" — a reference to the destructive artificial-intelligence system in the ""Terminator"" movies.
Whatever wider damage Burry had in mind, the financial stakes around the two companies are already enormous.

OpenAI and Anthropic didn't respond to requests for comment from Business Insider.
Both require substantial capital to develop advanced models and build the computing infrastructure that supports them. Going public could give them access to deeper pools of capital by opening them up to a wider range of investors.
Burry said in a Substack post this week that he was ""more confident than ever"" that his bearish AI thesis would play out over the next year, bringing forward his previous base case of 2028.

How IVF ‘add-on’ treatments put patients in an impossible position
There’s little evidence that many IVF “add-on” treatments work. Should they even be offered?
By Jackie Flynn Mogensen edited by Claire Cameron
Getty Images; Scientific American
This story discusses topics such as pregnancy loss.
Alessandra Dowling always wanted a big family. As one of four siblings, she hoped for three little ones of her own. When she found out she was pregnant in December 2022, after months of struggling with fertility, she was thrilled—she pictured sharing the news with family, meeting her baby and bringing her newborn home.
Then, in January 2023, she miscarried. “I was devastated,” Dowling, now age 33, recalls. “I told myself it was probably just bad luck because miscarriages are unfortunately common.” Indeed, around one in five pregnancies end in miscarriage. She still wanted a big family.
After a second miscarriage, “everything changed,” Dowling says. She stopped trusting her body. Every subsequent pregnancy test became associated with fear, not excitement. “I wasn’t just worried about getting pregnant anymore. I was terrified of losing another pregnancy,” she says.
If you're enjoying this article, consider supporting our award-winning journalism by subscribing. By purchasing a subscription you are helping to ensure the future of impactful stories about the discoveries and ideas shaping our world today.
Shortly after, she and her husband decided to turn to in vitro fertilization (IVF), a technique in which eggs are fertilized by sperm in a lab and then transferred to the uterus.
Dowling’s first egg retrieval was in March 2023; it was the beginning of what would be a long—and expensive—process. Insurance generally doesn’t cover IVF. Dowling and her husband paid out-of-pocket for most of their IVF procedures, as well as several “add-on” treatments that would supposedly boost her chances of delivering a baby. These included preimplantation genetic testing for aneuploidy (PGT-A) to detect any abnormalities in the embryos’ chromosomes; injections of human growth hormone to stimulate the ovaries; ozone therapy, an experimental treatment advertised as improving the odds of pregnancy; and medications to prevent blood clots and regulate her immune system. Dowling also received—and paid for—acupuncture before and throughout her IVF experience.
In all, she estimates she has spent close to $200,000 on fertility-related treatments.
Dowling’s experience isn’t unusual. In the U.S., IVF often costs individuals tens of thousands of dollars. And add-ons like the ones Dowling used are common; surveys conducted in the U.K. and Australia indicate that at least three quarters of IVF patients have tried them at one point or another in the course of their treatment. Yet in many cases, these treatments just aren’t backed by science.
A recent meta-analysis of 10 popular add-on treatments found that three treatments—endometrial receptivity testing (basically, testing of the uterine lining), corticosteroids (a category of immune system drugs) and PGT-A—had little to no impact on the odds of a live birth. Four add-ons—acupuncture, intralipid infusion (fat injections intended to regulate the immune system) and platelet-rich-plasma (PRP), or blood injections, in the uterus and ovaries—had either mixed evidence or low quality data backing them up.
Just three procedures had any evidence of a possible benefit, according to the review: endometrial scratching, a procedure similar to a biopsy, physiological intracytoplasmic sperm injection (PICSI), a method of selecting sperm, and EmbryoGlue, a type of treatment to help embryos attach to the uterus.
While the results don’t prove the treatments are ineffective, they reveal how many products are on the market with little to no evidence of helping people actually give birth to a baby. “Most add-ons are not proven to benefit patients going through IVF,” the study authors concluded.
That conclusion is no surprise to Dowling. She knew that many of the add-ons she used weren’t guaranteed to improve her chances of pregnancy or birth. Both she, a lawyer, and her husband, a physician, are data-oriented individuals, she says. They sifted through research repositories such as PubMed looking for clinical trials, consulted fertility experts and talked to other people who’d gone through the IVF process. Her doctors were transparent that some add-ons might help some people, while others, inexplicably, might see no benefit.
But as she struggled with her fertility, Dowling’s logic shifted. Turning down something that might help, even if the odds were low or unknowable, felt “impossible,” she says.
The add-ons helped her feel that she had “left no stone unturned,” she recalls. “By that point, I was very desperate to have a baby. We were willing to do anything and everything that might improve our chances.”
The reason why so many unproven treatments are available in the U.S. to begin with is murky. For one thing, the U.S. National Institutes of Health has historically restricted studies on IVF, particularly anything to do with the embryo, says Marcelle Cedars, director of the Center for Reproductive Health at the University of California, San Francisco. As a result, research is being done outside of academia—where investigators rely on federal funding—and in an environment “much more driven by market forces,” she says.
That means IVF treatment may be motivated by profit, with clinics being forced to compete over their fertility offerings. And because IVF is generally not covered by insurance, patients aren’t necessarily eager to spend time signing up for clinical trials to test new treatments. Instead they want to pursue the “best” course of action, even if doctors aren’t sure what that looks like, Cedars says.
Alessandra Dowling and her family.
Courtesy of Alessandra Dowling
Dowling had her first child, her son Joey, in August 2024 via IVF.
Before that fateful round, she had consulted a reproductive immunologist and discovered that she had lupus anticoagulant, an antibody associated with abnormal blood clotting and recurrent pregnancy loss. Thinking this may be related to her fertility struggles, Dowling opted to add three medications to her IVF protocol: Lovenox to help reduce abnormal blood clotting; prednisone, a corticosteroid to suppress a negative immune response to an embryo; and an immune regulation medication called Neupogen. Doctors also discovered, and removed, excess tissue in her uterus called a uterine septum.
The round of IVF worked—although Dowling isn’t entirely sure why. Less than a year after her son’s birth, Dowling tried to get pregnant again, opting for the same add-ons as before—with no luck. “Maybe it was the embryo itself,” she says, referring to her successful pregnancy. “Maybe it was simply chance. There’s just no way to know.”
After that unsuccessful round, Dowling found herself considering new add-ons, including platelet-rich plasma and EmbryoGlue, for the next time. Then she got pregnant, conceiving spontaneously without IVF. Her daughter, Gemma, was born in April 2026.
Being a mom is better than Dowling hoped it would be. “It’s exhausting, but it is something that gives me so much happiness,” she says. “It was worth everything and then some.”
Dowling still dreams about having a third baby. For now, she plans to try to get pregnant without assistance. If she returns to IVF, she has two embryos left. If those don’t work, she says, she’s prepared to put IVF behind her. The process has been financially, physically and emotionally draining, she says. “It’s been years of this.”
She also doesn’t regret her add-on treatments. On one level, it didn’t matter whether they’d been vetted or not. At the time, they offered something she needed: peace of mind.
“You’re not necessarily paying for certainty,” she says. “You’re paying for hope. And when you’ve spent years trying to have a child, hope can be incredibly powerful and moving.”

Jackie Flynn Mogensen is a breaking news reporter at Scientific American. Before joining SciAm, she was a science reporter at Mother Jones, where she received a National Academies Eric and Wendy Schmidt Award for Excellence in Science Communications in 2024. Mogensen holds a master’s degree in environmental communication and a bachelor’s degree in earth sciences from Stanford University. She is based in New York City.

The MTA on Wednesday said President Donald Trump’s tariffs on foreign imports are on track to add $1 billion to the price of New York’s new train cars and buses.
The transit agency's vehicles are all built in the United States but rely on components manufactured abroad. MTA officials said during a board meeting that Trump’s tariffs on steel, aluminum, copper and other materials inflated building costs and threatened to “dilute the value” of the MTA’s five-year construction plan.
“Today’s tariffs on American-made rolling stock are diluting the American taxpayer’s investment by punishing manufacturers for sourcing components they cannot buy anywhere else,” said Jessie Lazarus, the head of the MTA’s rolling stock program.
Lazarus noted some of the components needed to build New York’s rail cars are so specialized they are only manufactured in countries like carbon steel from Japan, fan motors from Italy and electronic parts from Taiwan.
MTA officials said the agency is currently overseeing the purchase of $23 billion worth of train cars and buses, and the extra $1 billion reflects additional costs they expect to be imposed by the tariffs.
The price to build new subway cars has skyrocketed by 30% since Trump returned to office in January 2025, according to the MTA.
Gov. Kathy Hochul on Wednesday sent a letter to U.S. Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick demanding the White House exempt the MTA from any tariffs on components needed to build the MTA’s vehicles. She pointed out that the MTA complies with federal “Buy America” mandates requiring at least 70% of final products to be produced domestically.
“Donald Trump’s reckless trade war does more than just raise prices for New York families, it severely threatens government’s ability to deliver on big, ambitious projects,” Hochul said in a statement. “New York’s historic investments in mass transit include plans for the largest improvement to our rail and bus fleet in history, but Trump’s tariffs have created a $1 billion tax on transit and put these improvements at risk.”
During his 2024 campaign, Trump promised to impose sweeping tariffs on imported goods to spur domestic manufacturing. He has imposed tariffs on dozens of countries since returning to office, most of which have been struck down as unconstitutional by federal judges. He’s repeatedly imposed new tariffs after courts ruled previous ones were unlawful.
Lazarus said the MTA could avoid these new costs if the feds grant the exemption Hochul requested Wednesday.
“This exemption, if we’re able to achieve it, would allow us to continue our planned level of investment in American-made rolling stock,” she said.
When asked about Hochul's letter, White House spokesperson Olivia Wales did not say whether the federal government would give the MTA a tariff exemption but took the opportunity to criticize New York's transit system.
“Both MTA and New York have long been incompetent and poor stewards of taxpayer money – with mismanagement driving chronically overbudget and behind-schedule projects,"" Wales said in a statement. ""Democrats should stop attacking how President Trump is delivering historic trade and investment deals and instead focus on cleaning up their own act.”
This story was updated with a comment from a White House spokesperson.

68,2026-09-30,"Happy Wednesday! It's September 30, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.
In this morning's edition, we're looking at Jim Farley's fears of Chinese cars in the U.S., and Republicans figuring out how to ban those Chinese cars without banning Mercedes. We'll also look at China's electric trucks, and dealerships that love AI. 
If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.
Chinese cars have hit Europe, and European buyers are loving it. Competing automakers, though, are less enthused — take Ford CEO Jim Farley, for instance, who thinks the United States would be better off without such foreign competition. From Reuters: 
Sept 29 (Reuters) – Ford CEO Jim Farley said that the US must carefully weigh how and whether Chinese automakers should enter ​the American market, and pointed to Europe as a cautionary tale.
Farley ‌said at an Automotive News conference on Tuesday that the US must ""be extremely careful around how the Chinese OEMs come to our country,"" and cited the pressure ​on Europe's auto industry as Chinese imports quickly grab market share ​there.
For European countries, ""it's too late,"" he said.
Can you imagine the state of the U.S. auto industry if we'd banned imports of Japanese vehicles in the '70s? I thought we were supposed to be a capitalist nation, one where any betterment is meant to come through competition — are we saying we can't compete with China at all, and we have to legally cordon off a set of domestic buyers that have no choice but to keep buying our inferior products? Chinese cars are, in many cases, cheaper and better than domestic U.S. competition, but our domestic automakers seem to be throwing their hands up rather than actually meeting the new challenge. 
It seems Farley is getting his way, because a bill working its way through Congress right now bans automakers with more than 15% Chinese ownership from selling in the United States. There's just one problem: Many automakers we already have some sort of Chinese ownership, including Mercedes. From Reuters: 
WASHINGTON, Sept 29 (Reuters) – A sponsor of a bill to toughen a US government ban on Chinese vehicles said on Tuesday that talks are ongoing to ensure that German automaker ​Mercedes-Benz is not banned from selling vehicles in the US.
The bill approved by the Senate Commerce ‌Committee in July would ban companies with more than 15% ownership by Chinese entities from selling vehicles in the US. Mercedes-Benz has nearly 20% passive ownership from Chinese entities.
...
Moreno said there ​were discussions about how to ensure that Volvo Cars, majority owned by China's Geely Holding, could continue to sell ​vehicles in the US.
This is a bit of a mask-off move from our lawmakers, because they're making it clear that Chinese ownership isn't actually the issue. If Mercedes is fine with its 20% Chinese ownership — or, even more extreme, Volvo's Geely ownership — then what's the actual issue here? Legislators seem fine with the money spent on vehicles in the U.S. going to Chinese pockets, they just seem concerned that it not happen via a car with a Chinese badge on the hood. This is so dumb. 
Here in the United States, we love our smoke-spewing Peterbilts like we love our baseball and apple pie. Attempts to replace them with alternative fuels, via vehicles like the Tesla Semi or whatever Nikola was doing, have largely turned out to be vaporware. But in China, truckers are already going electric, and it's saved their skins in the face of rising oil prices. From Bloomberg: 
Qu is one of thousands of drivers powering a profound shift in the world's biggest oil import market. Chinese e-truck sales jumped almost 80% in the first half from a year earlier, according to BloombergNEF, getting a boost as the US-Iran war and Ukrainian attacks on Russian refineries sent diesel prices soaring. They are on pace to account for close to a third of all rigs sold this year, up from just 8% a couple of years ago.
Diesel consumption in China will drop more than 10% this year, Sinopec Economics & Development Research Institute estimates, a shift that has helped Beijing navigate this year's oil shock and limited price rises for global markets in turn — all while cutting emissions, even if coal still plays a major role in electricity generation.
The electrification of road freight is emerging as a structural force that could undermine long-term oil demand across the world.
Tesla still claims the Semi is coming at some point, but Tesla claims a lot of things that turn out to be pipe dreams. There are a couple other entrants into the electrified trucking space, but we've yet to see the kind of adoption here that China has. Maybe ever-rising diesel prices will start to change that. 
Dealerships love to adopt new technologies without really understanding why or how those technologies will help anyone, and it's the same story with AI. Over 80% of dealerships are using it, in spite of the data security risks, and while dealer owners are getting concerned about security they're not backing down from adoption. From Automotive News: 
DETROIT — Dealerships are cautiously moving forward with artificial intelligence in the wake of new warnings from the technology's developers that unchecked AI could have doomsday consequences.
Recent instances of AI chatbots breaking through their constraints have led developers to call for proper limits to its reach. Auto retailers, mindful of the benefits they could gain from adopting AI, also are including guardrails to protect their businesses and customers.
""It makes me very cautious,"" Jeff Laethem, president of Ray Laethem Buick-GMC in Detroit, told Automotive News at the Digital Dealer conference here.
""I do believe there are major dangers with AI if [it is] uncontrolled,"" he said. ""It's riding that fine line of making sure you're keeping up with the things that need to happen and putting in the proper safeguards to make sure that customer data is safe.""
...
More than 8 in 10 dealers said they are using AI in some capacity, according to Cox Automotive.
Does AI actually help dealerships do anything? There were examples early on of buyers negotiating deals with dealer chatbots, making handshake deals for $10 cars that the dealership staff then had to unwind, but has AI helped anyone buy a car since then? Dealer websites are already generally terrible, I remain unconvinced that adding large language models to the mix will help.

Prices keep dropping! Take a road trip immediately.

Tech > Mobile
MVNOs score higher than AT&T, T-Mobile and Verizon in many key categories.
Senior Consumer Insights Editor
Senior Consumer Insights Editor

				Edited by Nasha Addarich Martínez 
September 30, 2026, 1:53 pm ET
If you plan to upgrade your phone during upcoming early holiday sales, now is the time to think about how satisfied you are with your mobile carrier. Switching to a new carrier you may have never considered before could give you the best deals and service for your new phone. 
Major network carriers, like AT&T, Verizon and T-Mobile, are well known for having plenty of perks and programs, like the Apple Upgrade program that can come in handy if you’re considering the iPhone 18 Pro Max or iPhone Duo. But don’t overlook mobile virtual network operators. Many of them can be more affordable and flexible, while still offering top notch cellular service just like major network carriers.  
CNET’s People’s Picks asked readers what they think about their mobile carrier, and they have a few favorites. Surprisingly, readers’ top picks aren’t among the big three major network carriers and may make you think twice about which mobile carrier you trust for cellular service. 
More from CNET
CNET readers chose Mint Mobile as CNET’s People’s Picks overall mobile carrier winner. It has a strong overall satisfaction average rating of 4.68 out of 5.
“Customer satisfaction in general is a fuzzy metric, and Mint Mobile has done an excellent job of reinforcing to its customers that they’ve made the right choice,” said Jeff Carlson, CNET senior editor. “I chalk a lot of that up to its marketing, led by actor and former part-owner Ryan Reynolds, which elevates Mint in a crowded market. But Mint remains affordable and reliable, which solidifies its customers’ choice.”
Mint Mobile beats out other MVNOs and major network carriers, giving you the best bang for your buck. It offers flexibility without costing a fortune. 
Mint Mobile offers several phone plans depending on your term and data needs. It offers three, six and 12-month plans starting at $15 per month for some plans, but an upfront payment is required. Mint Mobile also offers family plans that let you choose the data amount for each included line at one set price. You’ll also have the option to use your existing phone or buy one through Mint Mobile.
“The position of Mint Mobile and the other MVNOs on this list really shows how much people value simplicity in pricing and features,” said Carlson. “Yes, Mint requires up-front payment to get the best value, but customers can see what that is. They can budget a set amount for the next six months, pay it, and then not worry about fluctuations during that time.”
Carlson also noted that Mint Mobile isn’t trying to load up its plans with features to justify higher costs. It has core features such as 5G coverage and high-speed data, which is what most people are looking for.
Comparatively, major networks struggle in this category. For comparison, Verizon and AT&T are 2.98 and 3.05, respectively. It’s also worth noting that readers are happy with Mint Mobile’s pricing — 90% say their bill has remained consistent over the past year, and 5% say it has decreased. 
Usually, if your carrier’s cellular data connection isn’t reliable enough, you’ll go looking for a Wi-Fi network. Most CNET readers with Mint Mobile service (70%) give the mobile carrier high ratings for how well they’re able to rely on Mint Mobile’s cellular data without connecting to a Wi-Fi network. 
Over half (59%) of CNET readers with Mint Mobile service rarely have that problem, and for some, not at all. Eleven percent of CNET readers with Mint Mobile service say they leave Wi-Fi off entirely because their cellular data is faster.
Carlson said it is surprising that MVNOs, including Mint Mobile, win against major network categories for cellular data connection, but there are many reasons people connect to Wi-Fi instead of relying on their mobile carrier — like downloading files quickly or watching YouTube videos. 
Consumer Cellular wins a few categories in CNET’s People’s Picks, including signal reliability, customer service and the mobile carrier that’s least likely to drop calls. It also comes close to winning in two categories: cellular data connection and unlimited data. 
Consumer Cellular wins in signal reliability, with the highest average rating of 4.60 out of 5. US Mobile and Mint Mobile trail closely behind, with averages of 4.56 and 4.53, respectively. 
But that’s not the whole story, because all the MVNOs use the big carriers’ infrastructure – they don’t have their own cell towers. Most are tied to a single carrier, like Mint Mobile, which uses T-Mobile’s network. On its website, Consumer Cellular says, “When you sign up for our service, your network is assigned based on the strongest signal available in your geographic area.” Consumer Cellular’s customers likely give it a high score because they can use the network that works best in their area.
If you’re curious about coverage, Consumer Cellular has an interactive map that shows which areas are covered based on your zip code or address. Most areas have 5G or 4G LTE, and Wi-Fi calling is also available. 
If you’re constantly dealing with dropped calls, CNET readers with Consumer Cellular give it high marks compared to other mobile carriers. Most (70%) of Consumer Cellular readers say they rarely or never lose service. Close to 23% experience call drops in rural areas and on road trips — the lowest among mobile carriers. Even fewer experience call drops inside their home. Carlson said that could be because CNET readers with the service are getting the best coverage in their area, which is also why readers have high signal reliability ratings.
Consumer Cellular also wins in customer service, with an average rating of 4.72 out of 5, compared to US Mobile’s 4.34. There are two ways to reach Consumer Cellular for assistance: call (888) 345-5509 or use its online chat feature. Consumer Cellular also receives high rankings from CNET readers for its service and fast resolution times. Most say they were able to resolve issues with Consumer Cellular over the phone in fewer than 30 minutes. 
T-Mobile is the most popular mobile carrier among CNET readers (29%), followed by Verizon (18%). T-Mobile has several phone plans to choose from, but CNET editors recommend the T-Mobile Essentials Saver 2.0, which starts at $55 per month if you need only one line; the T-Mobile Experience More 2.0 may be ideal if you’re looking to save money from other perks. We like that T-Mobile’s autopay discount qualifies you for a lower price when you use an eligible payment method.
T-Mobile has a few features that make it worth considering if you’re looking for a new mobile carrier. CNET editors say it has a robust 5G network and some money-saving perks, like plan savings with a new line and T-Mobile Tuesdays — the carrier’s weekly appreciation program. And T-Mobile recently unveiled a three-year phone installment plan to make new phones more affordable. 
“One advantage T-Mobile has right now is its visibility – customers respond to T-Mobile Tuesdays, its sponsorship of things like a large section of the Formula One Las Vegas Grand Prix and its broad marketing,” said Carlson. “It feels like a place to be, and its mobile plans are well-received. They aren’t the cheapest, but their position here makes me think people are responding to T-Mobile giving them plenty of features for what they’re paying for.”
US Mobile earns a nearly perfect average of 4.65 out of 5 from CNET readers who use it, making it the highest among popular mobile carriers. MVNO competitors Mint Mobile and Consumer Cellular come close to winning this category, with average ratings of 4.63 and 4.48, respectively. 
Several factors go into determining data speeds. If you own a phone that’s capable of the highest 5G speeds and you’re in an area that can deliver them, you’ll see the best performance. That could be a device such as an iPhone 17 connected within a pocket of T-Mobile’s 5G Ultra Capacity (its name for 5G millimeter-wave spectrum) signal, which can download files much faster or deliver movies at 4K resolution smoothly.
Like Consumer Cellular, US Mobile isn’t tied to a specific carrier for its infrastructure. It’s able to use the best network available in your area – but US Mobile lets you choose which one to use. For example, if you travel to a location with a stronger network (like an extended vacation or for work), you can switch to that network for a fee. It uses its own names for the networks: Dark Star is AT&T, Light Speed is T-Mobile, and Warp is Verizon. 
US Mobile’s ability to leverage the best network in your area likely gives it the edge in this category. US Mobile’s Broadband Facts break down each plan’s data speeds. All three Unlimited plans (Flex, Starter and Premium) have unlimited data, but your download and upload speed will depend on which cellular network you’re connected to. US Mobile’s Dark Star has download speeds up to 389 Mbps, while the Light Speed and Warp networks have download speeds up to 357 Mbps and 702 Mbps, respectively. Upload speeds are much different. Dark Star, Light Speed and Warp have upload speeds up to 33, 32 and 702 Mbps. 
Fair pricing eliminates month-to-month surprises — giving you predictability and peace of mind when paying your bill. That’s why US Mobile also wins for CNET’s Fair Pricing category of People’s Picks, with 98% of US Mobile users saying their bill “remained perfectly consistent” or “actually went down” in the last 12 months. While other mobile carriers reported base monthly prices increasing, US Mobile didn’t report any new fees, perks going away or changes. 
US Mobile’s plans start at $25 per month for the Unlimited Starter plan, including taxes and fees, whereas the Unlimited Flex is an annual plan that starts at $17.50 per month, including taxes and fees.
Visible by Verizon gets the highest marks from readers who use the mobile service for its no-data-throttling policy. CNET found that 73.3% of Visible by Verizon users say their data is truly unlimited and has never been throttled, whereas 72.9% of readers with Consumer Cellular service say the same. Comparatively, 61% of Verizon postpaid users say the same. You may notice slower internet speeds if you reach your monthly high-speed data limit. 
Visible by Verizon offers unlimited data across all three plans, with premium (high-speed) data included on Visible Plus and Visible Plus Pro. Customers don’t need to worry about hitting a cap and seeing their speed dropped (though Visible and all carriers reserve the right to slow data if needed). While the Visible plan is listed as the most popular, the Visible Plus and Visible Plus Pro plans also offer unlimited data when roaming in Mexico and Canada, but speeds drop after 2GB per day of use.
Most (63%) of readers who have Google Fi Wireless are satisfied with its roaming features, the highest among mobile carriers. There are no reports of dissatisfaction or frustration from CNET readers.
CNET’s director of content, Patrick Holland, says Google Fi is one of Google’s best-kept secrets. “Its competitive pricing, international roaming (at no extra charge) and flexible data plans are some of the main reasons that it’s been my carrier of choice for most of the past decade,” said Holland. 
Google Fi Wireless has a few standout roaming features among other competitors. International service is available with Google Fi Wireless’ Unlimited Premium and Unlimited Standard plans. The Unlimited Premium plan includes free texts and calls at 20 cents per minute. Some calls to other countries (over 50) are free. Google Fi Wireless also offers 50GB of high-speed data to over 200 destinations, with 256kbps thereafter. Many other providers offer minimal international features.
Did you agree with these findings? CNET’s People’s Picks is community-driven and helps us spread the word about the tech and services you love. Take our ongoing laptops survey to tell us what you love about yours. 
CNET commissioned Alchemer to conduct the People’s Picks survey to compile data. The survey was carried out online, and all figures are from Alchemer. The total sample comprised 1,198 adults. The figures have been weighted and are representative of all US adults (aged 18 plus). Fieldwork was undertaken July 1 to July 26, 2026.

71,2026-09-30,"European investors can now gain exposure to bitcoin without having to worry about exchange-rate risks eroding their returns.
London-based asset management platform HANetf has listed exchange-traded commodities (ETCs) that provide exposure to bitcoin while hedging swings in the pound sterling and euro against the U.S. dollar, HANetf said in an emailed announcement on Wednesday.
Multinational bank HSBC is providing the currency hedging for the products. 
“These are the the world’s first currency-hedged crypto ETCs, bringing a new currency hedged structure to the European crypto ETC market,” HANetf said in Wednesday’s announcement.
Unlike in the U.S., ETFs in the European Union (EU) and U.K. must hold a diversified basket of assets. ETCs, therefore, exist to provide exposure to a single commodity or commodity group.
HANetf’s Arrow Bitcoin GBP Hedged ETC (GBTC) lists on the London Stock Exchange while its euro counterpart (EBTC) lists on Frankfurt-based Xetra and Euronext Paris.
They are designed to address an inherent aspect of bitcoin that also applies to gold: it’s almost universally denominated in U.S. dollars. This means investors in markets like Europe and elsewhere also take on dollar exposure when investing in BTC.
HANetf is targeting investors who want long-term exposure to bitcoin, but may be concerned about potential dollar weakness. 
Currency-hedged gold ETCs are already a $23 billion asset class, approximately 13% of the gold ETC market in Europe, with HANetf providing products that hedge the euro, pound and Swiss franc. 
Cryptocurrency exchange-traded products (ETPs) - the umbrella term for ETFs, ETCs and exchange-traded notes (ETNs) - are a predominant vehicle for attracting investors to digital assets, giving them exposure to crypto without the worry of managing custody of the assets themselves. 
Investors more accustomed to traditional financial (TradFi) assets may demand products and services that replicate what they use in TradFi, and HANetf’s GBTC and EBTC are the latest examples.

72,2026-09-30,"There's no shortage of excellent streaming boxes on the market today, but the Apple TV has two key advantages that put it ahead of the competition: performance and software. Apple uses the same A-series processors you'll find in its smartphones and tablets inside Apple TV boxes, and it shows. On the software side, tvOS feels familiar to iOS, iPadOS, and macOS users. But more importantly, it's lighter on bloat and advertisements than, say, Google's Android TV or Samsung's Tizen OS. These two perks are exactly why I've been a loyal Apple TV user for years — until everything changed with one update.
Apple's controversial Liquid Glass update actually requires a good amount of processing power to execute properly. The visual rendering of Liquid Glass user interface elements hits older and lower-powered devices the hardest. I never questioned the capabilities of my years-old Apple TV 4K until I updated to tvOS 26. Everything felt slower following the update, and now I'm mulling my options. With no new Apple TV 4K on the market, will tvOS 27 optimize performance or make matters worse? 
For $30, I expected the Onn to turn me off, but I was pleasantly surprised
I'm usually the one that advocates for installing software updates as soon as they arrive. When a new update drops, I urge my friends and family to install it immediately, so that they benefit from the new features, optimizations, and security patches. There are a few exceptions, though. I typically hold off on installing major releases on mission-critical devices, like my laptop, that I depend on for work. It's better to wait a few weeks for bugs and issues to sort themselves out before I load the upgrade onto my primary workstation. 
I drag my feet installing Apple TV updates too, for a completely different reason. I don't need my streaming box to have the latest features or undergo major changes. I simply need it to reliably stream shows in the same way it always has. In my view, an Apple TV update can't meaningfully improve the streaming experience — but it can make things worse. That's why, up until recently, my Apple TV was running an ancient version of tvOS 16. 
My Apple TV streaming box was multiple major releases out of date, but it was still as reliable as ever. That was until I tried to watch the latest season of Reacher on Amazon Prime Video and encountered error after error. The app needed an update, and it wasn't compatible with my tvOS version. So, I reluctantly brought my Apple TV up to the latest software release, which was tvOS 26 at the time. The experience after updating was exactly why I held off updating my Apple TV for so long. 
Installing tvOS 26 on my Apple TV 4K brought Liquid Glass to my streaming box for the first time. Design preferences aside, it's fair to wonder whether an Apple TV really needs to waste system resources on rendering glassy visual UI elements. Apple is well aware of the performance demands of Liquid Glass, because the first-generation Apple TV 4K omits the redesign entirely. The A10X Fusion chip powering the original Apple TV 4K can't keep up with the graphical requirements of the new design language, so it skips it.
I have the second-generation Apple TV 4K, which is powered by a newer A12 Bionic system-on-a-chip. If you have the third-generation Apple TV 4K, you get an A15 Bionic chip that's better suited to handling Liquid Glass. However, the second-generation Apple TV 4K that I own is sort of an awkward middle child — it's powerful enough to run Liquid Glass, but it's not powerful enough to do it well. If I had a newer or older version of the Apple TV 4K, I'd be better off. 
Instead, my five-year-old Apple TV 4K stutters when navigating the home screen and settings menu on tvOS 26. Considering I had no problems before updating, I'm attributing these problems to Liquid Glass. I'm not the only one — multiple users on Reddit describe similar experiences. The problems specifically affect the second-gen Apple TV 4K. If you have an older model, your device dodges Liquid Glass entirely, avoiding the slowdown. Those with newer models have the hardware to render Liquid Glass without a performance dip. 
If your Apple TV is feeling slow after tvOS 26 or tvOS 27, there are some settings you can tweak to try and improve performance. A hidden toggle can be found under Settings → Accessibility → Motion → Reduce Motion. When the feature is turned on, tvOS will turn off unnecessary animations, which may make your streaming box feel faster. You can also go to Settings → Accessibility → Display → Reduce Transparency to limit the glassy effect of Liquid Glass, potentially improving performance. 
These changes might help, but they don't address the real problem: Apple added unnecessary design elements to tvOS that certain Apple TV models have difficulty rendering. While I might be inclined to consider a hardware upgrade, Apple doesn't have one. The newest model is still using an older A15 Bionic chip designed for the iPhone 13 series. It's already too far out of date for me to consider replacing my A12 Bionic version with a new A15 Bionic-powered Apple TV 4K. 
If Apple is going to bog down the Apple TV 4K with intensive software updates that you'll eventually have to install, it needs to give them better hardware. The current specs aren't cutting it.

                                    Windows 11 version 26H2 is now generally available
                                

                                    Surface feels more fragmented than ever after Microsoft's latest refresh
                                

                                    Windows 11 version 26H2: Everything you need to know

With Windows 11 version 26H1 and 26H2 being based on different Windows codebases, Microsoft is planning to bring everyone back onto the same platform release with version 27H2 in 2027.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Microsoft is yet to confirm plans for Windows 11 version 27H2, but that doesn't mean we don't yet know anything about it. Thanks to information supplied by Microsoft about Windows 11 version 26H1 and 26H2, we can read the tea leaves and begin to decipher what Microsoft's plans are for its OS in 2027.
First up, we must set the stage for Windows 11 currently. For the first time ever, Microsoft has split the Windows userbase in two. Most Windows 11 users will continue to receive OS updates that are based on the Germanium platform release, which powers version 24H2, version 25H2, and the new version 26H2.
Germanium has been in the market since 2024, and is what the majority of Windows 11 users are running today. That will no longer be the case with version 26H1, which is the first version of Windows 11 since 2024 to ship on a new version of the Windows platform. This new version is codenamed Bromine, and exists exclusively for Snapdragon X2 and RTX Spark devices.
That means version 26H1 and the newer Bromine platform release will not be made available to the existing Windows 11 userbase this year. Microsoft doesn't say why, but my sources tell me this is because Bromine only really exists because of timing convenience. These new Arm chips required extra kernel level work, and it was easier for Microsoft to build those into a new platform release than it was to retroactively fit the Germanium platform release with these additions.
Because of this, Microsoft hasn't made any optimizations to the Bromine platform release for existing PCs. While you can run Bromine on existing hardware if you want, there is no benefit in doing so. As a result, Microsoft chose to keep the majority of Windows users in the platform release that has been shipping since 2024, and split off the new Snapdragon X2 and RTX Spark PCs with their own platform release.
That does mean there's going to be some awkward fragmentation between version 26H1 and version 26H2 throughout 2026 and into 2027, however. Microsoft has confirmed that because version 26H1 is based on a newer platform release than version 26H2, those running 26H1 are not eligible for the version 26H2 release.
The company does say that this won't be the case forever, and that Windows 11 users running version 26H1 will be updated to something newer in the future. According to my sources, that will be version 27H2 in 2027, which will be the first platform release since 25H2 to be offered to the entire Windows 11 userbase.
 



As is almost always the case, Microsoft tends to ship new versions of Windows in the second half of the year. I anticipate this will remain the case for version 27H2, which will likely target a September or October release window.
Because version 27H2 is likely to introduce a new platform release, Microsoft will use a measured and phased rollout approach to its general availability. That means not all users will see the update right away once it's made available, as Microsoft will choose to roll it out in waves to ensure the update is installing as intended.
It will also be a full blown OS upgrade release, and not just an enablement package. That means it will replace the entire OS with a new version, while maintaining all your apps, files and settings. This takes a little longer than an enablement package release, which only updates a few OS files to enable the new version.
Windows 11 version 22H2 was a fill blown OS upgrade release over 21H2, as was version 24H2 over 23H2. So this wouldn't be the first time this has happened.
 



We know that the version 27H2 release will need to be based on a newer platform release than Germanium to support upgrading both 26H2 and 26H1 users. What we don't yet know is what platform release this will be. It's possible that Microsoft could use the Bromine platform release for 27H2, though I think this is unlikely.
I think Microsoft will issue a platform release that's newer than Bromine. Given the timing, I suspect it may be the Windows Strontium platform release, which is expected to be finalized sometime in the first half of 2027. This would align with Microsoft's previous development schedules.
For example, Germanium was finalized in the first half of 2024, and ended up shipping as version 24H2 in the second half of that year. With Strontium expected to be finalized in the first half of 2027, I would wager the same will happen then for version 27H2.
Microsoft has announced that the next version of Windows will drop support for the Snapdragon 850 SoC. While not confirmation on its own, it's a strong hint that version 27H2 will be powered by a newer platform release than Bromine, given the Snapdragon 850 can still run Bromine-based builds.
 



Because of Microsoft's ""Continuous Innovation"" effort, the company no longer ties new features to version updates. These days, Windows 11 receives new features whenever the company deems them ready to ship, meaning there's no features in active development that are expected to be exclusive to version 27H2 currently, though that could change in the future.
In fact, most mainline feature development still takes places on the Germanium codebase, then is forward integrated into newer codebases when ready. This is why new features often arrive on version 25H2 and 26H2 first, and then come to 26H1 later, as 26H1 is built on a newer Windows platform.
 



Of course, the age old question is whether or not the next version of Windows will be dubbed Windows 12. The answer is we don't know, and likely won't know until much closer to the time.
Even if Microsoft does debut Windows 12 next year, that doesn't mean Windows 11 version 27H2 wouldn't also exist. When Windows 11 version 21H2 debuted, Windows 10 still received its own 21H2 version that same year.
With all that in mind, I don't think we're going to see Windows 12 in 2026. Given the current versions of Windows 11 are split between two platform releases, it would be irresponsible and complex from a support perspective to leave the userbase in that state.
It would make the upgrade path from Windows 11 to Windows 12 more complex, and Microsoft would need to account for multiple in-market platform releases and versions. I would think it's better to get everyone back onto the same platform version under Windows 11 first, before trying to push forward with Windows 12.

It now offers hotel stays and experiences, trip insurance, consumer products, lounge access, points convertible for airline miles, and virtual cards.
